\section{Decorated Programs}



 The beauty of Hoare Logic is that it is \textit{syntax directed}: the
    structure of proofs exactly follows the structure of programs.


    We can record the essential ideas of a Hoare-logic proof --
    omitting low-level calculational details -- by ``decorating'' a
    program with appropriate assertions on each of its commands.


    Such a \textit{decorated program} carries within itself an argument for
    its own correctness. 

 For example, consider the program:


    X := m;
    Z := p;
    while X <> 0 do
      Z := Z - 1;
      X := X - 1
    end
 Here is one possible specification for this program, in the
    form of a Hoare triple:


    \footnote{\url{ True }}
    X := m;
    Z := p;
    while X <> 0 do
      Z := Z - 1;
      X := X - 1
    end
    \footnote{\url{ Z = p - m }}
 (Note the \textit{parameters} \coqdocvar{m} and \coqdocvar{p}, which stand for
   fixed-but-arbitrary numbers.  Formally, they are simply Rocq
   variables of type \coqexternalref{nat}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocinductive{nat}}.) 

 Here is a decorated version of this program, embodying a
    proof of this specification:


    \footnote{\url{ True }} ->>
    \footnote{\url{ m = m }}
      X := m
                         \footnote{\url{ X = m }} ->>
                         \footnote{\url{ X = m /\ p = p }};
      Z := p;
                         \footnote{\url{ X = m /\ Z = p }} ->>
                         \footnote{\url{ Z - X = p - m }}
      while X <> 0 do
                         \footnote{\url{ Z - X = p - m /\ X <> 0 }} ->>
                         \footnote{\url{ (Z - 1) - (X - 1) = p - m }}
        Z := Z - 1
                         \footnote{\url{ Z - (X - 1) = p - m }};
        X := X - 1
                         \footnote{\url{ Z - X = p - m }}
      end
    \footnote{\url{ Z - X = p - m /\ ~ (X <> 0) }} ->>
    \footnote{\url{ Z = p - m }}


 Concretely, a decorated program consists of the program's text
    interleaved with assertions (sometimes multiple assertions
    separated by ->>). 

 A decorated program can be viewed as a compact representation of a
    proof in Hoare Logic: the assertions surrounding each command
    specify the Hoare triple to be proved for that part of the program
    using one of the Hoare Logic rules, and the structure of the
    program itself shows how to assemble all these individual steps
    into a proof for the whole program. 

 Our goal is to verify such decorated programs ``mostly
    automatically.''  But, before we can verify anything, we need to be
    able to \textit{find} a proof for a given specification, and for this we
    need to discover the right assertions. This can be done in an
    almost mechanical way, with the exception of finding loop
    invariants. In the remainder of this section, we explain in detail
    how to construct decorations for several short programs, all of
    which are loop free or have simple loop invariants. We'll return
    to finding more interesting loop invariants later in the chapter. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
\subsection{Example: Swapping}



 Consider the following program, which swaps the values of two
    variables using addition and subtraction, instead of by assigning
    to a temporary variable.


       X := X + Y;
       Y := X - Y;
       X := X - Y


    We can give a proof, in the form of decorations, that this program is
    correct -- i.e., it really swaps \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} and \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} -- as follows.


    (1)    \footnote{\url{ X = m /\ Y = n }} ->>
    (2)    \footnote{\url{ (X + Y) - ((X + Y) - Y) = n /\ (X + Y) - Y = m }}
             X := X + Y
    (3)                     \footnote{\url{ X - (X - Y) = n /\ X - Y = m }};
             Y := X - Y
    (4)                     \footnote{\url{ X - Y = n /\ Y = m }};
             X := X - Y
    (5)    \footnote{\url{ X = n /\ Y = m }}


    The decorations can be constructed as follows:



\begin{itemize}
\item  We begin with the undecorated program (the unnumbered lines).



\item  We add the specification -- i.e., the outer precondition (1)
        and postcondition (5). In the precondition, we use parameters
        \coqdocvar{m} and \coqdocvar{n} to remember the initial values of variables \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}
        and \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} so that we can refer to them in the postcondition (5).



\item  We work backwards, mechanically, starting from (5) and
        proceeding until we get to (2). At each step, we obtain the
        precondition of the assignment from its postcondition by
        substituting the assigned variable with the right-hand-side of
        the assignment. For instance, we obtain (4) by substituting
        \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} with \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} - \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} in (5), and we obtain (3) by substituting \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}}
        with \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} - \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} in (4).



\item  Finally, we verify that (1) logically implies (2) -- i.e., that
        the step from (1) to (2) is a valid use of the law of
        consequence -- by doing a bit of high-school algebra.

\end{itemize}
 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
\subsection{Example: Simple Conditionals}



 Here is a simple decorated program using conditionals:


      (1)   \footnote{\url{ True }}
              if X <= Y then
      (2)                    \footnote{\url{ True /\ X <= Y }} ->>
      (3)                    \footnote{\url{ (Y - X) + X = Y \/ (Y - X) + Y = X }}
                Z := Y - X
      (4)                    \footnote{\url{ Z + X = Y \/ Z + Y = X }}
              else
      (5)                    \footnote{\url{ True /\ ~(X <= Y) }} ->>
      (6)                    \footnote{\url{ (X - Y) + X = Y \/ (X - Y) + Y = X }}
                Z := X - Y
      (7)                    \footnote{\url{ Z + X = Y \/ Z + Y = X }}
              end
      (8)   \footnote{\url{ Z + X = Y \/ Z + Y = X }}


These decorations can be constructed as follows:



\begin{itemize}
\item  We start with the outer precondition (1) and postcondition (8).



\item  Following the format dictated by the \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.hoare if}{\coqdoclemma{hoare\_if}} rule, we copy the
    postcondition (8) to (4) and (7). We conjoin the precondition (1)
    with the guard of the conditional to obtain (2). We conjoin (1)
    with the negated guard of the conditional to obtain (5).



\item  In order to use the assignment rule and obtain (3), we substitute
    \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Z}{\coqdocdefinition{Z}} by \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} - \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} in (4). To obtain (6) we substitute \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Z}{\coqdocdefinition{Z}} by \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} - \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}}
    in (7).



\item  Finally, we verify that (2) implies (3) and (5) implies (6). Both
    of these implications crucially depend on the ordering of \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} and
    \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} obtained from the guard. For instance, knowing that \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} \ensuremath{\le} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}}
    ensures that subtracting \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} from \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} and then adding back \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}
    produces \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}}, as required by the first disjunct of (3). Similarly,
    knowing that \ensuremath{\lnot} (\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} \ensuremath{\le} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}}) ensures that subtracting \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} from \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}
    and then adding back \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} produces \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}, as needed by the second
    disjunct of (6). Note that \coqdocvar{n} - \coqdocvar{m} + \coqdocvar{m} = \coqdocvar{n} does \textit{not} hold for
    arbitrary natural numbers \coqdocvar{n} and \coqdocvar{m} (for example, 3 - 5 + 5 =
    5). 
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Example: Reduce to Zero}



 Here is a \coqdocvar{while} loop that is so simple that \coqexternalref{True}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocinductive{True}} suffices
    as a loop invariant.


        (1)    \footnote{\url{ True }}
                 while X <> 0 do
        (2)                  \footnote{\url{ True /\ X <> 0 }} ->>
        (3)                  \footnote{\url{ True }}
                   X := X - 1
        (4)                  \footnote{\url{ True }}
                 end
        (5)    \footnote{\url{ True /\ ~(X <> 0) }} ->>
        (6)    \footnote{\url{ X = 0 }}


   The decorations can be constructed as follows:



\begin{itemize}
\item  Start with the outer precondition (1) and postcondition (6).



\item  Following the format dictated by the \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.hoare while}{\coqdoclemma{hoare\_while}} rule, we copy
       (1) to (4). We conjoin (1) with the guard to obtain (2). We also
       conjoin (1) with the negation of the guard to obtain (5).



\item  Because the final postcondition (6) does not syntactically match (5),
       we add an implication between them.



\item  Using the assignment rule with assertion (4), we trivially substitute
       and obtain assertion (3).



\item  We add the implication between (2) and (3).

\end{itemize}


   Finally we check that the implications do hold; both are trivial. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
\subsection{Example: Division}



 Let's do one more example of simple reasoning about a loop.


    The following Imp program calculates the integer quotient and
    remainder of parameters \coqdocvar{m} and \coqdocvar{n}.


       X := m;
       Y := 0;
       while n <= X do
         X := X - n;
         Y := Y + 1
       end;


    If we replace \coqdocvar{m} and \coqdocvar{n} by concrete numbers and execute the program, it
    will terminate with the variable \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} set to the remainder when \coqdocvar{m}
    is divided by \coqdocvar{n} and \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} set to the quotient. 

 In order to give a specification to this program we need to
    remember that dividing \coqdocvar{m} by \coqdocvar{n} produces a remainder \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} and a
    quotient \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} such that \coqdocvar{n} \ensuremath{\times} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} + \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = \coqdocvar{m} \ensuremath{\land} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} < \coqdocvar{n}.


    It turns out that we get lucky with this program and don't have to
    think very hard about the loop invariant: the loop invariant is just
    the first conjunct, \coqdocvar{n} \ensuremath{\times} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} + \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = \coqdocvar{m}, and we can use this to
    decorate the program.


      (1)  \footnote{\url{ True }} ->>
      (2)  \footnote{\url{ n * 0 + m = m }}
             X := m;
      (3)                     \footnote{\url{ n * 0 + X = m }}
             Y := 0;
      (4)                     \footnote{\url{ n * Y + X = m }}
             while n <= X do
      (5)                     \footnote{\url{ n * Y + X = m /\ n <= X }} ->>
      (6)                     \footnote{\url{ n * (Y + 1) + (X - n) = m }}
               X := X - n;
      (7)                     \footnote{\url{ n * (Y + 1) + X = m }}
               Y := Y + 1
      (8)                     \footnote{\url{ n * Y + X = m }}
             end
      (9)  \footnote{\url{ n * Y + X = m /\ ~ (n <= X) }} ->>
     (10)  \footnote{\url{ n * Y + X = m /\ X < n }}


    Assertions (4), (5), (8), and (9) are derived mechanically from
    the loop invariant and the loop's guard.  Assertions (8), (7), and (6)
    are derived using the assignment rule going backwards from (8)
    to (6).  Assertions (4), (3), and (2) are again backwards
    applications of the assignment rule.


    Now that we've decorated the program it only remains to check that
    the uses of the consequence rule are correct -- i.e., that (1)
    implies (2), that (5) implies (6), and that (9) implies (10). This
    is indeed the case:

\begin{itemize}
\item  (1) ->> (2):  trivial, by algebra.

\item  (5) ->> (6):  because \coqdocvar{n} \ensuremath{\le} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}, we are guaranteed that the
        subtraction in (6) does not get zero-truncated.  We can
        therefore rewrite (6) as \coqdocvar{n} \ensuremath{\times} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} + \coqdocvar{n} + \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} - \coqdocvar{n} and cancel the
        \coqdocvar{n}s, which results in the left conjunct of (5).

\item  (9) ->> (10):  if \ensuremath{\lnot} (\coqdocvar{n} \ensuremath{\le} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}) then \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} < \coqdocvar{n}.  That's
        straightforward from high-school algebra.

\end{itemize}
    So, we have a valid decorated program. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
\subsection{From Decorated Programs to Formal Proofs}



 From an informal proof in the form of a decorated program, it is
    ``easy in principle'' to read off a formal proof using the Rocq
    theorems corresponding to the Hoare Logic rules, but these proofs
    can be a bit long and fiddly. 

 Note that we do \textit{not} unfold the definition of \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.valid hoare triple}{\coqdocdefinition{valid\_hoare\_triple}}
    anywhere in this proof: the point of the game we're playing now
    is to use the Hoare rules as a self-contained logic for reasoning
    about programs. 

 For example...

\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Imp.::com scope:'<x7B' x 'x7D>'}{\coqdocnotation{<\{}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.:com:com scope:'while' x 'do' x 'end'}{\coqdocnotation{while}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.:com::x '<>' x}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\not=}}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{0}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.:com:com scope:'while' x 'do' x 'end'}{\coqdocnotation{do}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{3.50em}
\coqref{SECF.Imp.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.:com:com scope:x ':=' x}{\coqdocnotation{:=}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.:com::x '-' x}{\coqdocnotation{-}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{1}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{2.50em}
\coqref{SECF.Imp.:com:com scope:'while' x 'do' x 'end'}{\coqdocnotation{end}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.::com scope:'<x7B' x 'x7D>'}{\coqdocnotation{\}>}}.\coqdoceol

In \coqdoclibrary{Hoare} we introduced a series of tactics named
    \coqdocvar{assertion\_auto} to automate proofs involving assertions.


    The following declaration introduces a more sophisticated tactic
    that will help with proving assertions throughout the rest of this
    chapter.  You don't need to understand the details, but briefly:
    it uses \coqdoctac{split} repeatedly to turn all the conjunctions into
    separate subgoals, tries to use several theorems about booleans
    and (in)equalities, then uses \coqdoctac{eauto} and \coqdocvar{lia} to finish off as
    many subgoals as possible. What's left after \coqdocvar{verify\_assertion} does
    its thing should be just the ``interesting parts'' of the proof
    (which, if we're lucky, might be nothing at all!).

This example shows that it is conceptually straightforward to read
    off the main elements of a formal proof from a decorated program.
    Indeed, the process is so straightforward that it can be
    automated, as we will see next. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
\section{Formal Decorated Programs}



 Our informal conventions for decorated programs amount to a
    way of ``displaying'' Hoare triples, in which commands are annotated
    with enough embedded assertions that checking the validity of a
    triple is reduced to simple logical and algebraic calculations
    showing that some assertions imply others.


    In this section, we show that this presentation style can be made
    completely formal -- and indeed that checking the validity of
    decorated programs can be largely automated.  
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
\subsection{Syntax}



 The first thing we need to do is to formalize a variant of the
    syntax of Imp commands that includes embedded assertions, which
    we'll call ``decorations.''  We call the new commands \textit{decorated
    commands}, or \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.dcom}{\coqdocinductive{dcom}}s.


    The choice of exactly where to put assertions in the definition of
    \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.dcom}{\coqdocinductive{dcom}} is a bit subtle.  The simplest thing to do would be to
    annotate every \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.dcom}{\coqdocinductive{dcom}} with a precondition and postcondition --
    something like this... 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Module} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare2.DComFirstTry}{DComFirstTry}{\coqdocmodule{DComFirstTry}}.\coqdoceol

\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqdoceol

But this would result in \textit{very} verbose decorated programs with a
    lot of repeated annotations: a simple program like
    \coqdocvar{skip};\coqdocvar{skip} would be decorated like this,


        \footnote{\url{P}} (\footnote{\url{P}} skip \footnote{\url{P}}) ; (\footnote{\url{P}} skip \footnote{\url{P}}) \footnote{\url{P}}


    with pre- and post-conditions around each \coqdocvar{skip}, plus identical
    pre- and post-conditions on the semicolon! 

 In other words, we don't want both preconditions and
    postconditions on each command, because a sequence of two commands
    would contain redundant decorations--the postcondition of the
    first likely being the same as the precondition of the second.


    Instead, our formal syntax of decorated commands will omit
    preconditions whenever possible and embed just postconditions. 


\begin{itemize}
\item  The \coqdocvar{skip} command, for example, is decorated only with its
      postcondition


      skip \footnote{\url{ Q }}


      on the assumption that the precondition will be provided by
      somebody else.


      We carry the same assumption through the other syntactic forms:
      each decorated command is assumed to carry its own postcondition
      within itself but take its precondition from its context in
      which it is used. 
\end{itemize}



\begin{itemize}
\item  Sequences \coqdocvar{d1} ; \coqdocvar{d2} need no additional decorations.


      Why?


      Because inside \coqdocvar{d2} there will be a postcondition, which also
      serves as the postcondition of \coqdocvar{d1};\coqdocvar{d2}.


      Similarly, inside \coqdocvar{d1} there will also be a postcondition, which
      additionally serves as the \textit{precondition} for \coqdocvar{d2}. 
\end{itemize}



\begin{itemize}
\item  An assignment \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} := \coqdocvar{a} is decorated only with its postcondition:


      X := a \footnote{\url{ Q }}

\end{itemize}



\begin{itemize}
\item  A conditional \coqdockw{if} \coqdocvar{b} \coqdockw{then} \coqdocvar{d1} \coqdockw{else} \coqdocvar{d2} is decorated with a
      postcondition for the entire statement, as well as preconditions
      for each branch:


      if b then \footnote{\url{ P1 }} d1 else \footnote{\url{ P2 }} d2 end \footnote{\url{ Q }}

\end{itemize}



\begin{itemize}
\item  A loop \coqdocvar{while} \coqdocvar{b} \coqdoctac{do} \coqdocvar{d} \coqdockw{end} is decorated with its final
      postcondition plus a precondition for the body:


      while b do \footnote{\url{ P }} d end \footnote{\url{ Q }}


      The postcondition embedded in \coqdocvar{d} serves as the loop invariant. 
\end{itemize}



\begin{itemize}
\item  Implications ->> can be added as decorations either for a
      precondition...



\begin{itemize}
\item >> \footnote{\url{ P }} d



\end{itemize}
      ...or for a postcondition:


      d ->> \footnote{\url{ Q }}


      The former is waiting for another precondition to be supplied by
      the context; the latter relies on the postcondition already
      embedded in \coqdocvar{d}. 
\end{itemize}


 Putting this all together gives us the formal syntax of decorated
    commands:

To provide the initial precondition that goes at the very top of a
    decorated program, we introduce a new type \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.decorated}{\coqdocinductive{decorated}}: 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Inductive} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare2.decorated}{decorated}{\coqdocinductive{decorated}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Type}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:=}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{|}}} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare2.Decorated}{Decorated}{\coqdocconstructor{Decorated}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.Assertion}{\coqdocdefinition{Assertion}} \coqexternalref{::type scope:x '->' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\rightarrow}}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.dcom}{\coqdocinductive{dcom}} \coqexternalref{::type scope:x '->' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\rightarrow}}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.decorated:51}{\coqdocinductive{decorated}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
To avoid clashing with the existing \coqdockw{Notation}s for ordinary
    commands, we introduce these notations in a new grammar scope
    called \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.dcom}{\coqdocinductive{dcom}}.

    notation is desugared.

\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Set Printing All}.\coqdoceol

\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Unset Printing All}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}

We can then express what it means for a decorated program to be
    correct as follows: 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent

The outer Hoare triple of a decorated program is just a \coqdockw{Prop};
    thus, to show that it is \textit{valid}, we need to produce a proof of
    this proposition.


    We will do this by extracting ``proof obligations'' from the
    decorations sprinkled throughout the program.


    These obligations are often called \textit{verification conditions},
    because they are the facts that must be verified to see that the
    decorations are locally consistent and thus constitute a proof of
    validity of the outer triple. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
\subsection{Extracting Verification Conditions}



 The function \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.verification conditions}{\coqdocdefinition{verification\_conditions}} takes a decorated command
    \coqdocvar{d} together with a precondition \coqdocvar{P} and returns a \textit{proposition}
    that, if it can be proved, implies that the triple


     \footnote{\url{P}} erase d \footnote{\url{post d}}


    is valid.


    It does this by walking over \coqdocvar{d} and generating a big conjunction
    that includes



\begin{itemize}
\item  local consistency checks for each form of command, plus



\item  uses of ->> to bridge the gap between the assertions found
      inside a decorated command and the assertions imposed by the
      external precondition; these uses correspond to applications
      of the consequence rule. 
\end{itemize}


 \textit{Local consistency} is defined as follows... 


\begin{itemize}
\item  The decorated command


        skip \footnote{\url{Q}}


      is locally consistent with respect to a precondition \coqdocvar{P} if
      \coqdocvar{P} ->> \coqdocvar{Q}.

\end{itemize}



\begin{itemize}
\item  The sequential composition of \coqdocvar{d1} and \coqdocvar{d2} is locally
      consistent with respect to \coqdocvar{P} if \coqdocvar{d1} is locally consistent with
      respect to \coqdocvar{P} and \coqdocvar{d2} is locally consistent with respect to
      the postcondition of \coqdocvar{d1}. 
\end{itemize}



\begin{itemize}
\item  An assignment


        X := a \footnote{\url{Q}}


      is locally consistent with respect to a precondition \coqdocvar{P} if:


        P ->> Q \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} |-> \coqdocvar{a}

\end{itemize}



\begin{itemize}
\item  A conditional


        if b then \footnote{\url{P1}} d1 else \footnote{\url{P2}} d2 end \footnote{\url{Q}}


      is locally consistent with respect to precondition \coqdocvar{P} if


         (1) \coqdocvar{P} \ensuremath{\land} \coqdocvar{b} ->> \coqdocvar{P1}


         (2) \coqdocvar{P} \ensuremath{\land} \ensuremath{\lnot}\coqdocvar{b} ->> \coqdocvar{P2}


         (3) \coqdocvar{d1} is locally consistent with respect to \coqdocvar{P1}


         (4) \coqdocvar{d2} is locally consistent with respect to \coqdocvar{P2}


         (5) \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.post}{\coqdocdefinition{post}} \coqdocvar{d1} ->> \coqdocvar{Q}


         (6) \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.post}{\coqdocdefinition{post}} \coqdocvar{d2} ->> \coqdocvar{Q}

\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}
\item  A loop


        while b do \footnote{\url{Q}} d end \footnote{\url{R}}


      is locally consistent with respect to precondition \coqdocvar{P} if:


         (1) \coqdocvar{P} ->> \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.post}{\coqdocdefinition{post}} \coqdocvar{d}


         (2) \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.post}{\coqdocdefinition{post}} \coqdocvar{d} \ensuremath{\land} \coqdocvar{b} ->> \coqdocvar{Q}


         (3) \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.post}{\coqdocdefinition{post}} \coqdocvar{d} \ensuremath{\land} \ensuremath{\lnot}\coqdocvar{b} ->> \coqdocvar{R}


         (4) \coqdocvar{d} is locally consistent with respect to \coqdocvar{Q}

\end{itemize}



\begin{itemize}
\item  A command with an extra assertion at the beginning



\begin{itemize}
\item >> \footnote{\url{Q}} d



\end{itemize}
      is locally consistent with respect to a precondition \coqdocvar{P} if:


        (1) \coqdocvar{P} ->> \coqdocvar{Q}


        (2) \coqdocvar{d} is locally consistent with respect to \coqdocvar{Q}

\end{itemize}



\begin{itemize}
\item  A command with an extra assertion at the end


         d ->> \footnote{\url{Q}}


      is locally consistent with respect to a precondition \coqdocvar{P} if:


        (1) \coqdocvar{d} is locally consistent with respect to \coqdocvar{P}


        (2) \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.post}{\coqdocdefinition{post}} \coqdocvar{d} ->> \coqdocvar{Q}

\end{itemize}


 With all this in mind, we can write a \textit{verification condition
    generator} that takes a decorated command and reads off a
    proposition saying that all its decorations are locally
    consistent.


    Formally, since a decorated command is ``waiting for its
    precondition'' the main VC generator takes a \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.dcom}{\coqdocinductive{dcom}} plus a given
    preondition as arguments.

The following key theorem states that \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.verification conditions}{\coqdocdefinition{verification\_conditions}}
    does its job correctly.  Not surprisingly, each of the Hoare Logic
    rules plays a critical role at some point in the proof.

\coqdocindent{1.00em}

Now that all the pieces are in place, we can define what it means
    to verify an entire program. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Definition} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare2.verification conditions from}{verification\_conditions\_from}{\coqdocdefinition{verification\_conditions\_from}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{7.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{(}}\coqdef{SECF.Hoare2.dec:72}{dec}{\coqdocbinder{dec}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.decorated}{\coqdocinductive{decorated}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{)}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Prop}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:=}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{match}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.dec:72}{\coqdocvariable{dec}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{with}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{|}}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.Decorated}{\coqdocconstructor{Decorated}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{P}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{d}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\Rightarrow}}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.verification conditions}{\coqdocdefinition{verification\_conditions}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{P}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{d}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{end}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
And this brings us to the main theorem of this section: 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Corollary} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare2.verification conditions correct}{verification\_conditions\_correct}{\coqdoclemma{verification\_conditions\_correct}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\forall}}} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare2.dec:74}{dec}{\coqdocbinder{dec}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{,}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare2.verification conditions from}{\coqdocdefinition{verification\_conditions\_from}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.dec:74}{\coqdocvariable{dec}} \coqexternalref{::type scope:x '->' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\rightarrow}}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare2.outer triple valid}{\coqdocdefinition{outer\_triple\_valid}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.dec:74}{\coqdocvariable{dec}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocnoindent
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Proof}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{intros}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{[}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{P}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{d}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{]}}. \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{apply}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.verification correct}{\coqdoclemma{verification\_correct}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocnoindent
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Qed}}.\coqdoceol

\subsection{More Automation}



 The propositions generated by \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.verification conditions}{\coqdocdefinition{verification\_conditions}} are fairly
    big and contain many conjuncts that are essentially trivial. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Eval} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{simpl}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{in}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.verification conditions from}{\coqdocdefinition{verification\_conditions\_from}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.dec while}{\coqdocdefinition{dec\_while}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
Fortunately, our \coqdocvar{verify\_assertion} tactic can generally take care of
    most (or sometimes all) of them. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Example} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare2.vc dec while}{vc\_dec\_while}{\coqdocdefinition{vc\_dec\_while}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.verification conditions from}{\coqdocdefinition{verification\_conditions\_from}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.dec while}{\coqdocdefinition{dec\_while}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocnoindent
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Proof}}. \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{verify\_assertion}}. \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Qed}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
To automate the overall process of verification, we can use
    \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.verification correct}{\coqdoclemma{verification\_correct}} to extract the verification conditions, use
    \coqdocvar{verify\_assertion} to verify them as much as it can, and finally tidy
    up any remaining bits by hand.  
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Ltac} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{verify}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:=}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{intros}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{;}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{apply}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.verification correct}{\coqdoclemma{verification\_correct}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{;}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{verify\_assertion}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
Here's the final, formal proof that dec\_while is correct. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Theorem} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare2.dec while correct}{dec\_while\_correct}{\coqdoclemma{dec\_while\_correct}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare2.outer triple valid}{\coqdocdefinition{outer\_triple\_valid}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.dec while}{\coqdocdefinition{dec\_while}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocnoindent
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Proof}}. \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{verify}}. \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Qed}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}

\section{Finding Loop Invariants}



 Once the outermost precondition and postcondition are
    chosen, the only creative part of a verifying program using Hoare
    Logic is finding the right loop invariants.  The reason this is
    difficult is the same as the reason that inductive mathematical
    proofs are:



\begin{itemize}
\item  Strengthening a \textit{loop invariant} means that you have a stronger
      assumption to work with when trying to establish the
      postcondition of the loop body, but it also means that the loop
      body's postcondition is harder to prove.



\item  Similarly, strengthening an \textit{induction hypothesis} means that
      you have a stronger assumption to work with when trying to
      complete the induction step of the proof, but it also means that
      the statement being proved inductively is harder to prove.

\end{itemize}


    This section explains how to approach the challenge of finding
    loop invariants through a series of examples and exercises. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
\subsection{Example: Slow Subtraction}



 The following program subtracts the value of \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} from the value of
    \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} by repeatedly decrementing both \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} and \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}}.  We want to verify its
    correctness with respect to the pre- and postconditions shown:


           \footnote{\url{ X = m /\ Y = n }}
             while X <> 0 do
               Y := Y - 1;
               X := X - 1
             end
           \footnote{\url{ Y = n - m }}


 To verify this program, we need to find an invariant \coqdocvar{Inv} for the
    loop.  As a first step we can leave \coqdocvar{Inv} as an unknown and build a
    \textit{skeleton} for the proof by applying the rules for local
    consistency, working from the end of the program to the beginning,
    as usual, and without doing any thinking at all yet. 

 This leads to the following skeleton:


        (1)    \footnote{\url{ X = m /\ Y = n }}  ->>                   (a)
        (2)    \footnote{\url{ Inv }}
                 while X <> 0 do
        (3)              \footnote{\url{ Inv /\ X <> 0 }}  ->>          (c)
        (4)              \footnote{\url{ Inv [X |-> X-1] [Y |-> Y-1] }}
                   Y := Y - 1;
        (5)              \footnote{\url{ Inv [X |-> X-1] }}
                   X := X - 1
        (6)              \footnote{\url{ Inv }}
                 end
        (7)    \footnote{\url{ Inv /\ ~ (X <> 0) }}  ->>                (b)
        (8)    \footnote{\url{ Y = n - m }}
 Examining this skeleton, we can see that any valid \coqdocvar{Inv} will
    have to respect three conditions:

\begin{itemize}
\item  (a) it must be \textit{weak} enough to be implied by the loop's
      precondition, i.e., (1) must imply (2);

\item  (b) it must be \textit{strong} enough to imply the program's postcondition,
      i.e., (7) must imply (8);

\item  (c) it must be \textit{preserved} by a single iteration of the loop, assuming
      that the loop guard also evaluates to true, i.e., (3) must imply (4). 
\end{itemize}


 These conditions are actually independent of the particular
    program and specification we are considering: every loop
    invariant has to satisfy them.


    One way to find a loop invariant that simultaneously satisfies these
    three conditions is by using an iterative process: start with a
    ``candidate'' invariant (e.g., a guess or a heuristic choice) and
    check the three conditions above; if any of the checks fails, try
    to use the information that we get from the failure to produce
    another -- hopefully better -- candidate invariant, and repeat.


    For instance, in the reduce-to-zero example above, we saw that,
    for a very simple loop, choosing \coqexternalref{True}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocinductive{True}} as a loop invariant did the
    job.  Maybe it will work here too.  To find out, let's try
    instantiating \coqdocvar{Inv} with \coqexternalref{True}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocinductive{True}} in the skeleton above and
    see what we get...


        (1)    \footnote{\url{ X = m /\ Y = n }} ->>                    (a - OK)
        (2)    \footnote{\url{ True }}
                 while X <> 0 do
        (3)                   \footnote{\url{ True /\ X <> 0 }} ->>     (c - OK)
        (4)                   \footnote{\url{ True }}
                   Y := Y - 1;
        (5)                   \footnote{\url{ True }}
                   X := X - 1
        (6)                   \footnote{\url{ True }}
                 end
        (7)    \footnote{\url{ True /\ ~(X <> 0) }} ->>                 (b - WRONG!)
        (8)    \footnote{\url{ Y = n - m }}


    While conditions (a) and (c) are trivially satisfied,
    (b) is wrong: it is not the case that \coqexternalref{True}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocinductive{True}} \ensuremath{\land} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = 0 (7)
    implies \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} = \coqdocvar{n} - \coqdocvar{m} (8).  In fact, the two assertions are
    completely unrelated, so it is very easy to find a counterexample
    to the implication (say, \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} = \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = \coqdocvar{m} = 0 and \coqdocvar{n} = 1).


    If we want (b) to hold, we need to strengthen the loop invariant so
    that it implies the postcondition (8).  One simple way to do
    this is to let the loop invariant \textit{be} the postcondition.  So let's
    return to our skeleton, instantiate \coqdocvar{Inv} with \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} = \coqdocvar{n} - \coqdocvar{m}, and
    try checking conditions (a) to (c) again.


    (1)    \footnote{\url{ X = m /\ Y = n }} ->>                        (a - WRONG!)
    (2)    \footnote{\url{ Y = n - m }}
             while X <> 0 do
    (3)                     \footnote{\url{ Y = n - m /\ X <> 0 }} ->>  (c - WRONG!)
    (4)                     \footnote{\url{ Y - 1 = n - m }}
               Y := Y - 1;
    (5)                     \footnote{\url{ Y = n - m }}
               X := X - 1
    (6)                     \footnote{\url{ Y = n - m }}
             end
    (7)    \footnote{\url{ Y = n - m /\ ~(X <> 0) }} ->>                (b - OK)
    (8)    \footnote{\url{ Y = n - m }}


    This time, condition (b) holds trivially, but (a) and (c) are
    broken. Condition (a) requires that (1) \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = \coqdocvar{m} \ensuremath{\land} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} = \coqdocvar{n}
    implies (2) \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} = \coqdocvar{n} - \coqdocvar{m}.  If we substitute \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} by \coqdocvar{n} we have to
    show that \coqdocvar{n} = \coqdocvar{n} - \coqdocvar{m} for arbitrary \coqdocvar{m} and \coqdocvar{n}, which is not
    the case (for instance, when \coqdocvar{m} = \coqdocvar{n} = 1).  Condition (c) requires
    that \coqdocvar{n} - \coqdocvar{m} - 1 = \coqdocvar{n} - \coqdocvar{m}, which fails, for instance, for \coqdocvar{n} = 1
    and \coqdocvar{m} = 0. So, although \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} = \coqdocvar{n} - \coqdocvar{m} holds at the end of the loop,
    it does not hold from the start, and it doesn't hold on each
    iteration; it is not a correct loop invariant.


    This failure is not very surprising: the variable \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} changes
    during the loop, while \coqdocvar{m} and \coqdocvar{n} are constant, so the assertion
    we chose didn't have much chance of being a loop invariant!


    To do better, we need to generalize (7) to some statement that is
    equivalent to (8) when \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} is 0, since this will be the case
    when the loop terminates, and that ``fills the gap'' in some
    appropriate way when \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} is nonzero.  Looking at how the loop
    works, we can observe that \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} and \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} are decremented together
    until \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} reaches 0.  So, if \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = 2 and \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} = 5 initially,
    after one iteration of the loop we obtain \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = 1 and \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} = 4;
    after two iterations \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = 0 and \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} = 3; and then the loop stops.
    Notice that the difference between \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} and \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} stays constant
    between iterations: initially, \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} = \coqdocvar{n} and \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = \coqdocvar{m}, and the
    difference is always \coqdocvar{n} - \coqdocvar{m}.  So let's try instantiating \coqdocvar{Inv} in
    the skeleton above with \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} - \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = \coqdocvar{n} - \coqdocvar{m}.


    (1)    \footnote{\url{ X = m /\ Y = n }} ->>                            (a - OK)
    (2)    \footnote{\url{ Y - X = n - m }}
             while X <> 0 do
    (3)                    \footnote{\url{ Y - X = n - m /\ X <> 0 }} ->>   (c - OK)
    (4)                    \footnote{\url{ (Y - 1) - (X - 1) = n - m }}
               Y := Y - 1;
    (5)                    \footnote{\url{ Y - (X - 1) = n - m }}
               X := X - 1
    (6)                    \footnote{\url{ Y - X = n - m }}
             end
    (7)    \footnote{\url{ Y - X = n - m /\ ~(X <> 0) }} ->>                (b - OK)
    (8)    \footnote{\url{ Y = n - m }}


    Success!  Conditions (a), (b) and (c) all hold now.  (To
    verify (c), we need to check that, under the assumption that
    \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} \ensuremath{\not=} 0, we have \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} - \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = (\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} - 1) - (\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} - 1); this holds for all
    natural numbers \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} and \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}}.)


    Here is the final version of the decorated program: 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Example} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare2.subtract slowly dec}{subtract\_slowly\_dec}{\coqdocdefinition{subtract\_slowly\_dec}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{(}}\coqdef{SECF.Hoare2.m:83}{m}{\coqdocbinder{m}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqexternalref{nat}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocinductive{nat}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{)}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{(}}\coqdef{SECF.Hoare2.n:84}{n}{\coqdocbinder{n}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqexternalref{nat}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocinductive{nat}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{)}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.decorated}{\coqdocinductive{decorated}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:=}}\coqdoceol

\subsection{Exercise: Slow Assignment}



\paragraph{Exercise: 2 stars, standard (slow\_assignment)}




    A roundabout way of assigning a number currently stored in \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} to
    the variable \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} is to start \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} at 0, then decrement \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} until
    it hits 0, incrementing \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} at each step. Here is a program that
    implements this idea.  Fill in decorations and prove the decorated
    program correct. (The proof should be very simple.)

\subsection{Example: Parity}



 Here is a cute way of computing the parity of a value initially
    stored in \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}, due to Daniel Cristofani.


       \footnote{\url{ X = m }}
         while 2 <= X do
           X := X - 2
         end
       \footnote{\url{ X = parity m }}


    The \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.parity}{\coqdocdefinition{parity}} function used in the specification is defined in
    Rocq as follows: 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Fixpoint} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare2.parity}{parity}{\coqdocdefinition{parity}} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare2.x:89}{x}{\coqdocbinder{x}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:=}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{match}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.x:89}{\coqdocvariable{x}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{with}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}

The postcondition does not hold at the beginning of the loop,
    since \coqdocvar{m} = \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.parity}{\coqdocdefinition{parity}} \coqdocvar{m} does not hold for an arbitrary \coqdocvar{m}, so we
    cannot hope to use that as a loop invariant.  To find a loop invariant
    that works, let's think a bit about what this loop does.  On each
    iteration it decrements \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} by 2, which preserves the parity of \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}.
    So the parity of \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} does not change, i.e., it is invariant.  The initial
    value of \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} is \coqdocvar{m}, so the parity of \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} is always equal to the
    parity of \coqdocvar{m}. Using \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.parity}{\coqdocdefinition{parity}} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.parity}{\coqdocdefinition{parity}} \coqdocvar{m} as an invariant we
    obtain the following decorated program:


      \footnote{\url{ X = m }} ->>                                         (a - OK)
      \footnote{\url{ parity X = parity m }}
        while 2 <= X do
                     \footnote{\url{ parity X = parity m /\ 2 <= X }} ->>  (c - OK)
                     \footnote{\url{ parity (X-2) = parity m }}
          X := X - 2
                     \footnote{\url{ parity X = parity m }}
        end
      \footnote{\url{ parity X = parity m /\ ~(2 <= X) }} ->>              (b - OK)
      \footnote{\url{ X = parity m }}


    With this loop invariant, conditions (a), (b), and (c) are all
    satisfied. For verifying (b), we observe that, when \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} < 2, we
    have \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.parity}{\coqdocdefinition{parity}} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} (we can easily see this in the definition of
    \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.parity}{\coqdocdefinition{parity}}).  For verifying (c), we observe that, when 2 \ensuremath{\le} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}, we
    have \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.parity}{\coqdocdefinition{parity}} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.parity}{\coqdocdefinition{parity}} (\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}-2). 

\paragraph{Exercise: 3 stars, standard, optional (parity)}




    Translate the above informal decorated program into a formal one
    and prove it correct.


    Hint: There are actually several possible loop invariants that all
    lead to good proofs; one that leads to a particularly simple proof
    is \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.parity}{\coqdocdefinition{parity}} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.parity}{\coqdocdefinition{parity}} \coqdocvar{m} -- or more formally, using the
    \# syntax to lift the application of the \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.parity}{\coqdocdefinition{parity}} function
    into the syntax of assertions, \{\{ \#\coqref{SECF.Hoare2.parity}{\coqdocdefinition{parity}} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = \#\coqref{SECF.Hoare2.parity}{\coqdocdefinition{parity}} \coqdocvar{m} \}\}.

\subsection{Example: Finding Square Roots}



 The following program computes the integer square root of \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}
    by naive iteration:


    \footnote{\url{ X=m }}
      Z := 0;
      while (Z+1)*(Z+1) <= X do
        Z := Z+1
      end
    \footnote{\url{ Z*Z<=m /\ m<(Z+1)*(Z+1) }}


 As we did before, we can try to use the postcondition as a
    candidate loop invariant, obtaining the following decorated program:


    (1)  \footnote{\url{ X=m }} ->>                  (a - second conjunct of (2) WRONG!)
    (2)  \footnote{\url{ 0*0 <= m /\ m<(0+1)*(0+1) }}
            Z := 0
    (3)            \footnote{\url{ Z*Z <= m /\ m<(Z+1)*(Z+1) }};
            while (Z+1)*(Z+1) <= X do
    (4)            \footnote{\url{ Z*Z<=m /\ m<(Z+1)*(Z+1)
                             /\ (Z+1)*(Z+1)<=X }} ->>          (c - WRONG!)
    (5)            \footnote{\url{ (Z+1)*(Z+1)<=m /\ m<((Z+1)+1)*((Z+1)+1) }}
              Z := Z+1
    (6)            \footnote{\url{ Z*Z<=m /\ m<(Z+1)*(Z+1) }}
            end
    (7)  \footnote{\url{ Z*Z<=m /\ m<(Z+1)*(Z+1) /\ ~((Z+1)*(Z+1)<=X) }} ->>    (b - OK)
    (8)  \footnote{\url{ Z*Z<=m /\ m<(Z+1)*(Z+1) }}


    This didn't work very well: conditions (a) and (c) both failed.
    Looking at condition (c), we see that the second conjunct of (4)
    is almost the same as the first conjunct of (5), except that (4)
    mentions \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} while (5) mentions \coqdocvar{m}. But note that \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} is never
    assigned in this program, so we should always have \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}=\coqdocvar{m}. We
    didn't propagate this information from (1) into the loop
    invariant, but we could!


    Also, we don't need the second conjunct of (8), since we can
    obtain it from the negation of the guard -- the third conjunct
    in (7) -- again under the assumption that \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}=\coqdocvar{m}.  This allows
    us to simplify a bit.


    So we now try \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}=\coqdocvar{m} \ensuremath{\land} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Z}{\coqdocdefinition{Z}}\ensuremath{\times}\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Z}{\coqdocdefinition{Z}} \ensuremath{\le} \coqdocvar{m} as the loop invariant:


    \footnote{\url{ X=m }} ->>                                           (a - OK)
    \footnote{\url{ X=m /\ 0*0 <= m }}
      Z := 0
                 \footnote{\url{ X=m /\ Z*Z <= m }};
      while (Z+1)*(Z+1) <= X do
                 \footnote{\url{ X=m /\ Z*Z<=m /\ (Z+1)*(Z+1)<=X }} ->>  (c - OK)
                 \footnote{\url{ X=m /\ (Z+1)*(Z+1)<=m }}
        Z := Z + 1
                 \footnote{\url{ X=m /\ Z*Z<=m }}
      end
    \footnote{\url{ X=m /\ Z*Z<=m /\ ~((Z+1)*(Z+1)<=X) }} ->>            (b - OK)
    \footnote{\url{ Z*Z<=m /\ m<(Z+1)*(Z+1) }}


    This works, since conditions (a), (b), and (c) are now all
    rather trivially satisfied.


    Very often, when a variable is used in a loop in a read-only
    fashion (i.e., it is referred to by the program or by the
    specification, and it is not changed by the loop), it is necessary
    to record the \textit{fact} that it doesn't change in the loop invariant. 

\paragraph{Exercise: 3 stars, standard, optional (sqrt)}




    Translate the above informal decorated program into a formal one
    and prove it correct.


    Hint: The loop invariant here must ensure that Z*Z is consistently
    less than or equal to X.

\subsection{Example: Squaring}



 Here is a program that squares \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} by repeated addition:


  \footnote{\url{ X = m }}
    Y := 0;
    Z := 0;
    while Y <> X  do
      Z := Z + X;
      Y := Y + 1
    end
  \footnote{\url{ Z = m*m }}


 The first thing to note is that the loop reads \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} but doesn't
    change its value. As we saw in the previous example, it can be a good idea
    in such cases to add \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = \coqdocvar{m} to the loop invariant.  The other thing
    that we know is often useful in the loop invariant is the postcondition,
    so let's add that too, leading to the candidate loop invariant
    \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Z}{\coqdocdefinition{Z}} = \coqdocvar{m} \ensuremath{\times} \coqdocvar{m} \ensuremath{\land} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = \coqdocvar{m}.


    \footnote{\url{ X = m }} ->>                                       (a - WRONG)
    \footnote{\url{ 0 = m*m /\ X = m }}
      Y := 0
                   \footnote{\url{ 0 = m*m /\ X = m }};
      Z := 0
                   \footnote{\url{ Z = m*m /\ X = m }};
      while Y <> X do
                   \footnote{\url{ Z = m*m /\ X = m /\ Y <> X }} ->>   (c - WRONG)
                   \footnote{\url{ Z+X = m*m /\ X = m }}
        Z := Z + X
                   \footnote{\url{ Z = m*m /\ X = m }};
        Y := Y + 1
                   \footnote{\url{ Z = m*m /\ X = m }}
      end
    \footnote{\url{ Z = m*m /\ X = m /\ ~(Y <> X) }} ->>               (b - OK)
    \footnote{\url{ Z = m*m }}


    Conditions (a) and (c) fail because of the \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Z}{\coqdocdefinition{Z}} = \coqdocvar{m}\ensuremath{\times}\coqdocvar{m} part.  While
    \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Z}{\coqdocdefinition{Z}} starts at 0 and works itself up to \coqdocvar{m}\ensuremath{\times}\coqdocvar{m}, we can't expect
    \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Z}{\coqdocdefinition{Z}} to be \coqdocvar{m}\ensuremath{\times}\coqdocvar{m} from the start.  If we look at how \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Z}{\coqdocdefinition{Z}} progresses
    in the loop, after the 1st iteration \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Z}{\coqdocdefinition{Z}} = \coqdocvar{m}, after the 2nd
    iteration \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Z}{\coqdocdefinition{Z}} = 2*\coqdocvar{m}, and at the end \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Z}{\coqdocdefinition{Z}} = \coqdocvar{m}\ensuremath{\times}\coqdocvar{m}.  Since the variable
    \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} tracks how many times we go through the loop, this leads us to
    derive a new loop invariant candidate: \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Z}{\coqdocdefinition{Z}} = \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}}\ensuremath{\times}\coqdocvar{m} \ensuremath{\land} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = \coqdocvar{m}.


    \footnote{\url{ X = m }} ->>                                        (a - OK)
    \footnote{\url{ 0 = 0*m /\ X = m }}
      Y := 0
                    \footnote{\url{ 0 = Y*m /\ X = m }};
      Z := 0
                    \footnote{\url{ Z = Y*m /\ X = m }};
      while Y <> X do
                    \footnote{\url{ Z = Y*m /\ X = m /\ Y <> X }} ->>   (c - OK)
                    \footnote{\url{ Z+X = (Y+1)*m /\ X = m }}
        Z := Z + X
                    \footnote{\url{ Z = (Y+1)*m /\ X = m }};
        Y := Y + 1
                    \footnote{\url{ Z = Y*m /\ X = m }}
      end
    \footnote{\url{ Z = Y*m /\ X = m /\ ~(Y <> X) }} ->>                (b - OK)
    \footnote{\url{ Z = m*m }}


    This new loop invariant makes the proof go through: all three
    conditions are easy to check.


    It is worth comparing the postcondition \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Z}{\coqdocdefinition{Z}} = \coqdocvar{m}\ensuremath{\times}\coqdocvar{m} and the
    \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Z}{\coqdocdefinition{Z}} = \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}}\ensuremath{\times}\coqdocvar{m} conjunct of the loop invariant. It is often the case
    that one has to replace parameters with variables -- or with
    expressions involving both variables and parameters, like
    \coqdocvar{m} - \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} -- when going from postconditions to loop invariants. 

 \ensuremath{\Box} 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
\subsection{Exercise: Factorial}



\paragraph{Exercise: 4 stars, advanced (factorial\_correct)}




    Recall that \coqdocvar{n}! denotes the factorial of \coqdocvar{n} (i.e., \coqdocvar{n}! =
    1*2*...*\coqdocvar{n}).  Formally, the factorial function is defined
    recursively in the Rocq standard library in a way that is
    equivalent to the following:


    Fixpoint fact (n : nat) : nat :=
      match n with
      | O => 1
      | S n' => n * (fact n')
      end.

\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Compute}} \coqdocdefinition{fact} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{5}}. \coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
First, write the Imp program \coqdocvar{factorial} that calculates the factorial
    of the number initially stored in the variable \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} and puts it in
    the variable \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}}. 

 Using your definition \coqdocvar{factorial} and \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.slow assignment dec}{\coqdocdefinition{slow\_assignment\_dec}} as a
    guide, write a formal decorated program \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.factorial dec}{\coqdocdefinition{factorial\_dec}} that
    implements the factorial function.  Hint: recall the use of \#
    in assertions to apply a function to an Imp variable.


    Fill in the blanks and finish the proof of correctness. Bear in mind
    that we are working with natural numbers, for which both division
    and subtraction can behave differently than with real numbers.
    Excluding both operations from your loop invariant is advisable!


    Then state a theorem named \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.factorial correct}{\coqdoclemma{factorial\_correct}} that says
    \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.factorial dec}{\coqdocdefinition{factorial\_dec}} is correct, and prove the theorem.  If all goes
    well, \coqdocvar{verify} will leave you with just two subgoals, each of
    which requires establishing some mathematical property of \coqdocdefinition{fact},
    rather than proving anything about your program.


    Hint: if those two subgoals become tedious to prove, give some
    thought to how you could restate your assertions such that the
    mathematical operations are more amenable to manipulation in Rocq.
    For example, recall that 1 + ... is easier to work with than
    ... + 1.

\coqdocnoindent
. \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Admitted}}.\coqdoceol

\subsection{Exercise: Minimum}



\paragraph{Exercise: 3 stars, advanced (minimum\_correct)}




    Fill in decorations for the following program and prove them
    correct.  As with \coqdocvar{factorial}, be careful about mathematical
    reasoning involving natural numbers, especially subtraction.


    Also, remember that applications of Rocq functions in assertions
    need an \coqdocvar{ap} or \coqdocvar{ap2} to be parsed correctly.  E.g., \coqdocdefinition{min} \coqdocvar{a} \coqdocvar{b}
    needs to be written \coqdocvar{ap2} \coqdocdefinition{min} \coqdocvar{a} \coqdocvar{b} in an assertion.


    You may find \coqdocvar{andb\_true\_eq} useful (perhaps after using symmetry

\subsection{Exercise: Two Loops}



\paragraph{Exercise: 3 stars, standard (two\_loops)}




    Here is a pretty inefficient way of adding 3 numbers:


     X := 0;
     Y := 0;
     Z := c;
     while X <> a do
       X := X + 1;
       Z := Z + 1
     end;
     while Y <> b do
       Y := Y + 1;
       Z := Z + 1
     end


    Show that it does what it should by completing the
    following decorated program.

\subsection{Exercise: Power Series}



\paragraph{Exercise: 4 stars, standard, optional (dpow2)}




    Here is a program that computes the series:
    1 + 2 + 2\^{}2 + ... + 2\^{}\coqdocvar{m} = 2\^{}(\coqdocvar{m}+1) - 1


    X := 0;
    Y := 1;
    Z := 1;
    while X <> m do
      Z := 2 * Z;
      Y := Y + Z;
      X := X + 1
    end


    Turn this into a decorated program and prove it correct.

\paragraph{Exercise: 2 stars, advanced, optional (fib\_eqn)}




    The Fibonacci function is usually written like this:


      Fixpoint fib n :=
        match n with
        | 0 => 1
        | 1 => 1
        | \_ => fib (pred n) + fib (pred (pred n))
        end.


   This doesn't pass Rocq's termination checker, but here is a
   slightly clunkier definition that does: 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Fixpoint} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare2.fib}{fib}{\coqdocdefinition{fib}} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare2.n:117}{n}{\coqdocbinder{n}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:=}}\coqdoceol

\paragraph{Exercise: 4 stars, advanced, optional (fib)}




    The following Imp program leaves the value of \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.fib}{\coqdocdefinition{fib}} \coqdocvar{n} in the
    variable \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} when it terminates:


    X := 1;
    Y := 1;
    Z := 1;
    while X <> 1 + n do
      T := Z;
      Z := Z + Y;
      Y := T;
      X := 1 + X
    end


    Fill in the following definition of \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.dfib}{\coqdocdefinition{dfib}} and prove that it
    satisfies this specification:


      \footnote{\url{ True }} dfib \footnote{\url{ Y = fib n }}


    You will need many uses of \coqdocvar{ap} in your assertions.
    If all goes well, your proof will be very brief.

\paragraph{Exercise: 5 stars, advanced, optional (improve\_dcom)}




    The formal decorated programs defined above are intended
    to look as similar as possible to the informal ones defined
    earlier.  If we drop this requirement, we can eliminate almost all
    annotations, just requiring final postconditions and loop
    invariants to be provided explicitly.  Do this -- i.e., define a
    new version of dcom with as few annotations as possible and adapt
    the rest of the formal development leading up to the
    \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.verification correct}{\coqdoclemma{verification\_correct}} theorem. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
\section{Weakest Preconditions (Optional)}



 Some preconditions are more interesting than others.
    For example, the Hoare triple


      \footnote{\url{ False }}  X := Y + 1  \footnote{\url{ X <= 5 }}


    is \textit{not} very interesting: although it is perfectly valid , it
    tells us nothing useful.  Since the precondition isn't
    satisfied by any state, it doesn't describe any situations where
    we can use the command \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} := \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} + 1 to achieve the postcondition
    \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} \ensuremath{\le} 5.


    By contrast,


      \footnote{\url{ Y <= 4 /\ Z = 0 }}  X := Y + 1 \footnote{\url{ X <= 5 }}


    has a useful precondition: it tells us that, if we can somehow
    create a situation in which we know that \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} \ensuremath{\le} 4 \ensuremath{\land} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Z}{\coqdocdefinition{Z}} = 0, then
    running this command will produce a state satisfying the
    postcondition.  However, this precondition is not as useful as it
    could be, because the \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Z}{\coqdocdefinition{Z}} = 0 clause in the precondition actually
    has nothing to do with the postcondition \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} \ensuremath{\le} 5.


    The \textit{most} useful precondition for this command is this one:


      \footnote{\url{ Y <= 4 }}  X := Y + 1  \footnote{\url{ X <= 5 }}


    The assertion \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} \ensuremath{\le} 4 is called the \textit{weakest precondition} of
    \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} := \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} + 1 with respect to the postcondition \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} \ensuremath{\le} 5. 

 Assertion \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} \ensuremath{\le} 4 is a \textit{weakest precondition} of command \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} :=
    \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} + 1 with respect to postcondition \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} \ensuremath{\le} 5.  Think of \textit{weakest}
    here as meaning ``easiest to satisfy'': a weakest precondition is
    one that as many states as possible can satisfy. 

 \coqdocvar{P} is a weakest precondition of command \coqdocvar{c} for postcondition \coqdocvar{Q}
    if



\begin{itemize}
\item  \coqdocvar{P} is a precondition, that is, \{\{\coqdocvar{P}\}\} \coqdocvar{c} \{\{\coqdocvar{Q}\}\}; and

\item  \coqdocvar{P} is at least as weak as all other preconditions, that is,
        if \{\{\coqdocvar{P'}\}\} \coqdocvar{c} \{\{\coqdocvar{Q}\}\} then \coqdocvar{P'} ->> \coqdocvar{P}.

\end{itemize}
 

 Note that weakest preconditions need not be unique.  For
    example, \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} \ensuremath{\le} 4 was a weakest precondition above, but so are the
    logically equivalent assertions \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} < 5, \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} \ensuremath{\le} 2 \ensuremath{\times} 2, etc.
    It is easy to show that any two weakest preconditions \coqdocvar{P} and \coqdocvar{P'}
    of a command \coqdocvar{c} with respect to postcondition \coqdocvar{Q} are logically
    equivalent; that is, \coqdocvar{P} <<->> \coqdocvar{P'}. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Definition} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare2.is wp}{is\_wp}{\coqdocdefinition{is\_wp}} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare2.P:124}{P}{\coqdocbinder{P}} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare2.c:125}{c}{\coqdocbinder{c}} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare2.Q:126}{Q}{\coqdocbinder{Q}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:=}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\{\{}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare2.P:124}{\coqdocvariable{P}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\}\}}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.c:125}{\coqdocvariable{c}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\{\{}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare2.Q:126}{\coqdocvariable{Q}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\}\}}} \coqexternalref{::type scope:x '/x5C' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\land}}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\forall}}} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare2.P':127}{P'}{\coqdocbinder{P'}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{,}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\{\{}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare2.P':127}{\coqdocvariable{P'}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\}\}}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.c:125}{\coqdocvariable{c}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\{\{}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare2.Q:126}{\coqdocvariable{Q}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\}\}}} \coqexternalref{::type scope:x '->' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\rightarrow}}} \coqexternalref{::type scope:x '->' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{(}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare2.P':127}{\coqdocvariable{P'}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:x '->>' x}{\coqdocnotation{->>}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.P:124}{\coqdocvariable{P}}\coqexternalref{::type scope:x '->' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{)}}.\coqdoceol

\paragraph{Exercise: 1 star, standard, optional (wp)}




    What are weakest preconditions of the following commands
    for the following postconditions?


  1) \footnote{\url{ ? }}  skip  \footnote{\url{ X = 5 }}


  2) \footnote{\url{ ? }}  X := Y + Z \footnote{\url{ X = 5 }}


  3) \footnote{\url{ ? }}  X := Y  \footnote{\url{ X = Y }}


  4) \footnote{\url{ ? }}
     if X = 0 then Y := Z + 1 else Y := W + 2 end
     \footnote{\url{ Y = 5 }}


  5) \footnote{\url{ ? }}
     X := 5
     \footnote{\url{ X = 0 }}


  6) \footnote{\url{ ? }}
     while true do X := 0 end
     \footnote{\url{ X = 0 }}

\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
\paragraph{Exercise: 3 stars, advanced, optional (is\_wp)}

    is indeed a weakest precondition of \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} := \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} + 1 with respect to
    postcondition \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} \ensuremath{\le} 5.


    Note: we have to put parentheses around the inputs to \coqref{SECF.Hoare2.is wp}{\coqdocdefinition{is\_wp}} to
    prevent Rocq from parsing those three things as a Hoare triple.
